Appendix A.  Using IDMEFv2 in Dynamic Environments

   1.0

   Proposal

   October 2026

A.1.  The Dynamic Asset Problem

   Modern container orchestration platforms and cloud runtime
   environments create and terminate resources dynamically.  IP
   addresses may change or be reassigned, resource names may be reused,
   and an entity involved in a security incident may no longer exist
   when an investigation takes place.

   Consequently, identifying a resource solely by its IP address or
   hostname is insufficient for reliable incident correlation and post-
   incident investigation.

   IDMEFv2 provides complementary attributes that can describe dynamic
   entities in the , , , and classes.  The attribute identifies an
   entity in the IDMEFv2 data model, while , , and provide additional
   information about its network presence, operational hostname, and
   infrastructure context.






Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 45]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   These attributes serve different purposes and SHOULD be used together
   when the relevant information is available.  They do not, by
   themselves, guarantee the long-term availability of historical
   records or the continued existence of the resource.

A.2.  Complementary Identification Attributes

   The following attributes have complementary roles when describing
   dynamic resources.

A.2.1.  ID

   The attribute identifies an entity in the IDMEFv2 data model.  It
   MUST be populated in accordance with the applicable IDMEFv2
   specification.

   An IDMEFv2 identifier and a platform-native identifier are different
   concepts.  For example, a Kubernetes pod UID identifies a Kubernetes
   object instance; it is not a substitute for the IDMEFv2 attribute.
   Implementations that need to correlate multiple alerts concerning the
   same resource SHOULD define a consistent mapping between the resource
   identity and the IDMEFv2 entity identifier.  They SHOULD also
   distinguish successive resource instances when a platform reuses an
   operational name.

   The identifier MUST NOT be assumed to provide access to a resource or
   to its historical metadata after termination.

A.2.2.  IP

   The attribute records the network address associated with the entity
   in the context of the event.

   It supports correlation with network telemetry, firewall logs, flow
   records, and other security monitoring data.  However, an IP address
   is a time-dependent network attribute and MUST NOT be treated as a
   persistent identifier of an dynamic resource.

   When available, the event timestamp and relevant network context
   SHOULD be retained to support the interpretation of the address at
   the time of the incident.

   Implementations SHOULD distinguish addresses belonging to different
   infrastructure layers, such as a pod, container, node, NAT gateway,
   or proxy, when this distinction is relevant to the investigation.






Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 46]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


A.2.3.  Hostname

   The attribute records the hostname observed or configured for the
   entity.  It SHOULD contain an actual hostname, not an unrelated
   platform identifier.

   For a Kubernetes pod, the hostname can be configured through the
   pod's hostname settings or obtained from the runtime environment.  A
   pod name and the hostname used inside the pod may happen to be the
   same, but they are not interchangeable by definition.
   Implementations SHOULD record the actual hostname observed or
   configured for the pod.

   Hostnames can facilitate operational identification and correlation
   during a resource's lifetime.  However, hostnames may be reused, and
   their uniqueness may be limited to a particular namespace, cluster,
   runtime, or time interval.  Consequently, SHOULD NOT be considered a
   globally unique or permanently stable identifier without additional
   context.

A.2.4.  Locator

   The attribute can carry a platform-specific resource reference that
   provides additional context for identifying an entity within its
   infrastructure.

   Depending on the environment, this reference MAY contain a cloud
   resource identifier, an orchestration resource path, a container
   identifier qualified by its runtime, or a combination of identifiers
   that uniquely identifies the relevant resource within its scope.

   Implementations SHOULD prefer references that identify the individual
   resource involved in the event rather than only its parent cluster,
   service, or workload definition.

   Where appropriate, a reference MAY include additional context, such
   as a cluster identifier, namespace, node identifier, container
   identifier, or resource UID.

   Implementations using platform-specific references SHOULD document
   their syntax, scope, uniqueness properties, and interpretation.

   A value does not, by itself, guarantee that a terminated resource can
   be retrieved or that its historical metadata remains available.
   Systems requiring post-incident traceability MUST retain the relevant
   historical metadata and security telemetry for the required
   investigation period.




Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 47]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


A.3.  Platform-Specific Resource References

   The examples below illustrate how can be populated in different
   environments.  The URI schemes used in these examples, including , ,
   , , and , are illustrative implementation conventions.  They are not
   defined or standardized by IDMEFv2.

   The values are illustrative and MUST be replaced with identifiers and
   hostnames obtained from the relevant platform in an actual
   deployment.  UUID-shaped values shown in the examples are
   illustrative IDMEFv2 entity identifiers; they are not Kubernetes UIDs
   unless explicitly placed in a platform reference.

A.3.1.  Kubernetes

   In Kubernetes, a pod name alone may not be sufficient to distinguish
   successive instances that share the same name.  The pod UID SHOULD be
   retained when available.

   A reference SHOULD include the cluster context, namespace, pod name,
   and pod UID.  Container-specific information MAY be added when the
   investigation concerns an individual container.

   The values below identify IDMEFv2 entities.  The values are
   illustrative actual hostnames configured or observed for the
   respective pods; they are not merely Kubernetes pod names.  The pod
   names and UIDs are retained in .

   Example:






















Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 48]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   {
     "CreateTime": "2026-10-19T10:15:30Z",
     "Analyzer": [
       {
         "ID": "7d6a3c21-1b4e-4f80-9a12-6e5b2c8d901f",
         "IP": "10.10.0.11",
         "Hostname": "secops-collector-01.example.net",
         "Locator": "k8s://cluster-secops/namespace-monitoring/pod/idmefv2-analyzer?uid=11111111-1111-4111-8111-111111111111"
       }
     ],
     "Sensor": [
       {
         "ID": "8e7b4d32-2c5f-4a91-8b23-7f6c3d9e012a",
         "IP": "10.10.0.12",
         "Hostname": "network-sensor-01.example.net",
         "Locator": "k8s://cluster-prod-eu/namespace-monitoring/pod/network-sensor?uid=22222222-2222-4222-8222-222222222222"
       }
     ],
     "Source": [
       {
         "ID": "9f8c5e43-3d60-4ba2-9c34-8a7d4e0f123b",
         "IP": "10.244.2.15",
         "Hostname": "payment-worker-01.namespace-paiement.example.net",
         "Locator": "k8s://cluster-prod-eu/namespace-paiement/pod/payment-worker-5c8d9f-abc123?uid=6a1f8b42-3c9d-4e70-a521-8b7d9c2e104f&container=worker&node=node-worker-03"
       }
     ],
     "Target": [
       {
         "ID": "a09d6f54-4e71-4cb3-8d45-9b8e5f10234c",
         "IP": "10.244.3.20",
         "Hostname": "payment-api-01.namespace-paiement.example.net",
         "Locator": "k8s://cluster-prod-eu/namespace-paiement/pod/payment-api-7b8c9d-xyz789?uid=33333333-3333-4333-8333-333333333333&container=api&node=node-worker-04",
         "Service": "finance"
       }
     ]
   }

   The reference is an illustrative convention.  The pod UID identifies
   the Kubernetes object instance, while the cluster and namespace
   provide the context required to interpret the reference.

   Node-level information MAY be included when it helps correlate the
   event with network sensors or host-level telemetry.








Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 49]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


A.3.2.  Docker and Podman

   For standalone container runtimes such as Docker and Podman, a
   container name alone may be insufficient because names can be reused
   after a container is removed.

   SHOULD identify the container instance and, where necessary, the
   runtime host or engine.  The full container identifier SHOULD be
   preferred over the human-readable container name.

   Example:

   {
     "Target": [
       {
         "ID": "b10e7a65-5f82-4dc4-9e56-0c9f6a21345d",
         "IP": "172.17.0.3",
         "Hostname": "web-app-01.example.net",
         "Locator": "docker://server-web-01/containers/a1b2c3d4e5f67890123456789012345678901234567890123456789012345678",
         "Service": "e-commerce"
       }
     ]
   }

   The scheme is an illustrative convention, not an official Docker URI
   scheme.  A corresponding convention MAY be defined for Podman, for
   example using a scheme.  Implementations SHOULD document whether the
   reference identifies the container, the runtime instance, the host,
   or a combination of these entities.

   Where multiple runtime hosts or engines are involved, the reference
   SHOULD include enough context to avoid ambiguity.

A.3.3.  Google Cloud GKE

   For Google Kubernetes Engine (GKE), a reference can combine the
   Google Cloud project and cluster context with the Kubernetes
   namespace, pod name, and pod UID.

   The cluster resource identifier alone identifies the cluster, not the
   individual pod involved in the incident.

   Example:








Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 50]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   {
     "Target": [
       {
         "ID": "c21f8b76-6083-4ed5-af67-1d0a7b32456e",
         "IP": "10.244.2.15",
         "Hostname": "payment-worker-01.namespace-paiement.example.net",
         "Locator": "gke://projects/my-project-123/locations/us-west1/clusters/cluster-prod-eu/namespaces/namespace-paiement/pods/payment-worker-5c8d9f-abc123?uid=6a1f8b42-3c9d-4e70-a521-8b7d9c2e104f&container=worker",
         "Service": "finance"
       }
     ]
   }

   The scheme is an illustrative convention rather than an official
   Google Cloud resource URI format.  Implementations MAY instead use a
   documented Google Cloud resource identifier together with the
   Kubernetes identifiers required to identify the individual pod.

A.3.4.  AWS ECS

   In Amazon Elastic Container Service (ECS), the task ARN identifies a
   task instance, whereas the task definition ARN identifies a versioned
   task definition.

   For incident investigation involving a particular running or
   terminated task, SHOULD identify the task instance rather than only
   its task definition.  The task definition ARN MAY be retained
   separately as supplementary configuration context.

   Example:

   {
     "Target": [
       {
         "ID": "d32f8a76-4b19-4c52-9d63-0a8e7f6b1234",
         "IP": "10.0.1.42",
         "Hostname": "payment-worker-01.example.net",
         "Locator": "arn:aws:ecs:us-west-1:123456789012:task/cluster-prod-eu/0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef",
         "Service": "finance"
       }
     ]
   }

   The ARN is illustrative.  Implementations MUST use the actual task
   ARN returned by ECS and SHOULD retain the cluster context when needed
   to interpret the reference.

   When the incident concerns an individual container within a task, the
   relevant container identifier SHOULD also be retained when available.



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 51]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   The task definition ARN MAY be recorded as additional information to
   identify the configuration used by the task.  It MUST NOT be
   substituted for the task instance identifier when the objective is to
   distinguish individual task executions.

A.3.5.  Microsoft Azure AKS

   In Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS), the Azure Resource Manager (ARM)
   resource ID of a managed cluster identifies the cluster, not an
   individual pod.

   A pod-level reference SHOULD therefore combine the Azure
   subscription, resource group, and cluster context with the Kubernetes
   namespace, pod name, and pod UID.

   Example:

   {
     "Target": [
       {
         "ID": "e43f9b87-5c20-4d63-8e74-1b9f6a20345d",
         "IP": "10.240.0.15",
         "Hostname": "payment-worker-01.namespace-paiement.example.net",
         "Locator": "aks://subscriptions/12345678-1234-1234-1234-123456789012/resourceGroups/my-rg/managedClusters/cluster-prod-eu/namespaces/namespace-paiement/pods/payment-worker-5c8d9f-abc123?uid=6a1f8b42-3c9d-4e70-a521-8b7d9c2e104f",
         "Service": "finance"
       }
     ]
   }

   The scheme is an illustrative convention, not an official ARM
   resource identifier for a pod.

   Implementations MAY retain the cluster ARM resource ID as a separate
   reference or incorporate it into a documented platform-specific
   representation.  The reference SHOULD distinguish the individual
   Kubernetes resource from its parent cluster.

A.3.6.  HashiCorp Nomad

   In HashiCorp Nomad, an allocation identifier can provide a reference
   to a particular workload allocation.  The reference SHOULD include
   sufficient context to distinguish allocations across clusters,
   regions, namespaces, or jobs, as applicable.

   Example:






Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 52]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   {
     "Target": [
       {
         "ID": "f54a0c98-6d31-4e75-9a82-2c7f1b60345e",
         "IP": "172.17.0.5",
         "Hostname": "payment-worker-01.example.net",
         "Locator": "nomad://region-eu/cluster-prod/job-payment/alloc-01234567-89ab-cdef-0123-456789abcdef?task=worker",
         "Service": "finance"
       }
     ]
   }

   The scheme is an illustrative convention.  Implementations SHOULD use
   the actual allocation identifier provided by Nomad and document the
   context required to interpret it.

   Task-level or container-level information MAY be included when
   necessary to distinguish the specific runtime entity involved in the
   event.

A.4.  Scope of the Service Attribute

   The attribute in the class SHOULD describe the high-level service or
   process affected, consistently with the IDMEFv2 specification.

   For example, values such as , , and can identify business-level
   services associated with the target.

   Implementations SHOULD avoid using as an undocumented substitute for
   platform-specific resource identifiers, such as pod names, container
   IDs, allocation IDs, task ARNs, or cluster resource IDs.

   Where business services and technical runtime services must be
   distinguished, implementations SHOULD document the mapping between
   these concepts and the relevant IDMEFv2 attributes.

   The use of for a particular purpose MUST remain consistent with the
   attribute definition in the applicable version of the IDMEFv2
   specification.

A.5.  Temporal Context and Historical Retention

   Reliable post-incident investigation requires more than a stable
   resource reference.







Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 53]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   Implementations SHOULD preserve the event timestamp and sufficient
   contextual information to determine which resource instance, network
   address, and runtime environment were involved at the time of the
   event.

   The IDMEFv2 time attributes SHOULD be used according to their defined
   semantics.  In particular, indicates when the alert was created,
   whereas describes the start of the activity or situation being
   reported when that information is available.  They are not
   interchangeable, and neither should be assumed to represent the exact
   measurement time of every individual attribute.  Implementations
   SHOULD retain any additional timestamps required to interpret time-
   sensitive measurements accurately.

   Systems requiring long-term traceability MUST retain the historical
   metadata and security telemetry needed for the intended investigation
   period.  Such information can include orchestration events, container
   lifecycle records, cloud audit logs, network flow records, and
   mappings between resource identifiers and network addresses.

   A value MAY be used as a lookup key into retained historical records.
   However, the availability and retention of those records are
   properties of the surrounding monitoring and logging infrastructure,
   not guarantees provided by the attribute itself.

A.6.  Summary

   Ephemeral environments require a distinction between entity
   identification, network correlation, and historical resource context.

   The attribute identifies the entity in the IDMEFv2 data model.
   records the network address observed in the context of the event,
   provides the actual configured or observed hostname, and can carry a
   platform-specific reference to the relevant resource.

   Implementations SHOULD use resource references that identify
   individual instances rather than only their parent clusters or
   configuration definitions.  They SHOULD preserve sufficient temporal
   and infrastructure context to distinguish successive instances and
   correlate events across security telemetry sources.

   Long-term traceability additionally depends on the retention and
   availability of historical metadata and security records.








Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 54]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   This approach enables IDMEFv2 to represent dynamic resources across
   different orchestration platforms and container runtimes without
   requiring a separate IDMEFv2 extension for each environment.
   Platform-specific reference formats remain implementation conventions
   and SHOULD be documented to support interoperability.

   The JSON fragments are illustrative.  Their exact structure MUST
   conform to the applicable IDMEFv2 serialization and schema.

Appendix B.  IDMEFv2 Alert Signature Specification

   1.0

   Proposal

   October 2026

   This appendix defines a standardized mechanism for signing IDMEFv2
   alerts.  The signature ensures the integrity and authenticity of
   alert data while allowing certain attributes to remain modifiable
   without invalidating the signature (partial signing).

   The mechanism builds upon the following standards:

   *  *JWS* (RFC 7515) — signature format;

   *  *JCS* (RFC 8785) — JSON canonicalization;

   *  *JWA* (RFC 7518) — cryptographic algorithms;

   *  *JWK* (RFC 7517) — key representation;

   *  *IDMEFv2 Paths* (Section 3.3.12 of the draft) — attribute
      selection.

   The key words *MUST*, *MUST NOT*, *SHOULD*, *SHOULD NOT*, *MAY* and
   *RECOMMENDED* are to be interpreted as described in RFC 2119.

B.1.  Overview

   The mechanism adds two optional attributes to the class:

   1.  *``SignedAttributes``*: a list of IDMEFv2 paths identifying the
       attributes to be signed;

   2.  *``Signature``*: a string containing the JWS Compact
       Serialization of the signature.




Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 55]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   The signing process follows these steps:

   1.  populate with the list of IDMEFv2 paths;

   2.  build the signed payload object as defined in Signed Attributes
       and Payload Construction (Appendix B.3);

   3.  canonicalize this object using JCS (RFC 8785);

   4.  build the protected JWS header (containing , and optionally );

   5.  produce the JWS Compact Serialization as defined in Signature
       Format: JWS Compact Serialization;

   6.  store the result in the attribute.

B.2.  Signature Format: JWS Compact Serialization

   The attribute *MUST* use the JWS Compact Serialization format defined
   in RFC 7515.

B.2.1.  Structure

   BASE64URL(JWS Protected Header) "." BASE64URL(JWS Payload) "." BASE64URL(JWS Signature)

   The JWS payload *MUST* be *embedded* (not detached) in the Compact
   serialization.  It corresponds to the canonicalized representation of
   the payload object defined in Signed Attributes and Payload
   Construction (Appendix B.3), encoded in base64url as specified by RFC
   7515.

   |  Note: The payload is derived from the alert itself; embedding it
   |  guarantees that the verifier has access to the exact
   |  representation that was signed, independently of any re-
   |  serialization of the alert.  The unencoded payload option of RFC
   |  7797 is *not* used.

B.2.2.  Protected Header

   The protected JWS header *MUST* contain the parameter.  It *SHOULD*
   contain the parameter.  It *MUST NOT* contain any parameter not
   specified by this annex.

B.2.3.  Example

   "Signature": "<valid JWS Compact string, to be provided in test vectors>"





Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 56]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


B.3.  Signed Attributes and Payload Construction

B.3.1.  Path Syntax

   Attributes to be signed are identified using the IDMEFv2 path syntax
   defined in Section 3.3.12 of the draft.

          +=================================+===================+
          | Attribute type                  | Example           |
          +=================================+===================+
          | Top-level attribute             |                   |
          +---------------------------------+-------------------+
          | Nested class attribute          |                   |
          +---------------------------------+-------------------+
          | List attribute (first element)  | (index 0 implied) |
          +---------------------------------+-------------------+
          | List attribute (specific index) |                   |
          +---------------------------------+-------------------+

                                  Table 6

B.3.2.  Payload Structure (Normative)

   The signed payload *MUST* be a *JSON object* whose structure mirrors
   the nesting of the paths listed in . Specifically:

   *  a top-level path produces a key at the root;

   *  a nested path produces an object containing a key ;

   *  a list path (or with an implied index 0) produces an *array* under
      the key , whose element at index 0 is an object containing the key
      ;

   *  list indices *MUST* be contiguous starting from 0 in the payload;
      unreferenced positions *MUST NOT* be included;

   *  *MUST* be included in the payload under the key , with the list of
      paths as provided (see Constraints on SignedAttributes
      (Appendix B.3.4));

   *  *MUST NOT* be included in the payload.

   *Example.* For , the payload is:







Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 57]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   {
     "Version": "2.D.V08",
     "ID": "123e4567-e89b-12d3-a456-426614174000",
     "Analyzer": { "Name": "Corporate EDR" },
     "Source": [ { "IP": "198.51.100.20" } ],
     "SignedAttributes": ["Version", "ID", "Analyzer.Name", "Source(0).IP"]
   }

B.3.3.  Absent or Invalid Attributes

   If a path listed in does not correspond to any attribute present in
   the alert, the producer *MUST* reject the signing operation
   (validation error).  A verifier that observes the absence of a
   referenced attribute *MUST* reject the signature.

B.3.4.  Constraints on SignedAttributes

   *  *MUST NOT* contain or .

   *  The order of paths in the list is *not significant* (see Attribute
      Order: Not Significant (Appendix B.5)).

   *  The list *MUST* be included in the payload to bind the signature
      to the covered set of paths.

B.4.  Attributes Added to the Alert Class

       +===========+======+=======================================+
       | Attribute | Type | Description                           |
       +===========+======+=======================================+
       |           |      | List of IDMEFv2 paths of the          |
       |           |      | attributes included in the signature. |
       +-----------+------+---------------------------------------+
       |           |      | JWS Compact Serialization string      |
       |           |      | containing the signature.             |
       +-----------+------+---------------------------------------+

                                 Table 7

B.4.1.  SignedAttributes

   Optional. *MUST* be present when is present. *MUST* be included in
   the JWS payload (see Payload Structure (Normative) (Appendix B.3.2)).

B.4.2.  Signature

   Optional.  Present only when the alert is signed.  Contains the
   complete JWS Compact Serialization.



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 58]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


B.4.3.  Consistency

   An alert that contains without , or vice versa, *MUST* be considered
   invalid by a verifier.

B.5.  Attribute Order: Not Significant

   The order of attributes in does not affect the signature because:

   1.  the payload is canonicalized using JCS (RFC 8785);

   2.  JCS deterministically sorts all keys in lexicographic order;

   3.  the same logical object therefore always produces the same
       canonical representation.

       Note: The array in the payload preserves the order of the
       specified indices; JCS does not reorder array elements.

B.6.  Normalization: JSON Canonicalization Scheme (JCS)

   All JSON objects used as the JWS payload *MUST* be canonicalized
   using JCS (RFC 8785) before signing and before verification.

B.6.1.  JCS Rules

   *  object keys are sorted lexicographically;

   *  whitespace outside string literals is removed;

   *  numbers are formatted according to I-JSON rules (RFC 7493);

   *  , and are forbidden.

B.6.2.  Rationale

   Without canonicalization, the same logical JSON object could be
   represented differently (key ordering, whitespace), leading to
   signature verification failures.  JCS ensures deterministic
   serialization.

B.7.  Signature Algorithms

B.7.1.  Supported Algorithms

   The following algorithms *MUST* be supported:





Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 59]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


         +===========+===========================+==============+
         | Algorithm | Type                      | Usage        |
         +===========+===========================+==============+
         |           | ECDSA P-256 / SHA-256     | RECOMMENDED  |
         +-----------+---------------------------+--------------+
         |           | RSASSA-PSS / SHA-256      | RECOMMENDED  |
         +-----------+---------------------------+--------------+
         | (Ed25519) | EdDSA curve 25519         | RECOMMENDED  |
         +-----------+---------------------------+--------------+
         |           | RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 / SHA-256 | IF NECESSARY |
         +-----------+---------------------------+--------------+

                                 Table 8

B.7.2.  Excluded Algorithms

   The following algorithms *MUST NOT* be used:

   *  (unsecured);

   *  , , (HMAC — inappropriate for third-party verification);

   *  any algorithm not listed in JWA (RFC 7518).

B.7.3.  Verification Policy

   A verifier *MUST* check that the value of the protected header
   corresponds to an algorithm allowed by its local policy, and *MUST*
   reject the signature otherwise (protection against algorithm
   confusion).

B.8.  Key Management

B.8.1.  Key Identification

   *``alg`` parameter.* *MUST* be included in the protected JWS header.
   It identifies the cryptographic algorithm used to secure the JWS.
   The signature is not valid if does not represent a supported
   algorithm, or if no key associated with the signing party is
   available for that algorithm.  The value *MUST* be a case-sensitive
   string containing a registered algorithm name (from the IANA "JSON
   Web Signature and Encryption Algorithms" registry as defined in JWA)
   or a value that contains a Collision-Resistant Name.








Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 60]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   *``kid`` parameter.* *SHOULD* be included in the protected JWS
   header.  It provides a hint indicating which key was used to secure
   the JWS, allowing the recipient to locate the verification key and
   facilitating key rotation.  Other identification mechanisms (out-of-
   band agreement) *MAY* be used, which is why this parameter remains
   optional.

   Example header:

   { "alg": "ES256", "kid": "key-2025-01" }

B.8.2.  Key Representation

   Keys *SHOULD* be represented in JWK format (RFC 7517) when exchanged
   as part of the payload.  Other mechanisms, such as referencing a key
   via a understood within a pre-existing PKI, *MAY* be used to maintain
   compatibility with existing infrastructures.

B.8.3.  Key Distribution

   The key distribution mechanism is outside the scope of this annex.
   The following methods *MAY* be used: pre-shared public keys, PKI /
   X.509 certificates, OAuth 2.0 or OpenID Connect metadata endpoints,
   manual exchange.

B.9.  Verification Procedure

   A verifier *MUST* apply the following steps:

   1.  check for the joint presence of and (Consistency
       (Appendix B.4.3)); reject otherwise;

   2.  decode the JWS Compact serialization and check that it has
       exactly three segments;

   3.  check that the protected header contains and that its value is
       allowed by the local policy (Verification Policy
       (Appendix B.7.3)); reject otherwise;

   4.  locate the verification key using or another mechanism (Key
       Management (Appendix B.8));

   5.  rebuild the payload object from the alert and as defined in
       Payload Structure (Normative) (Appendix B.3.2); if a referenced
       attribute is absent, reject (Absent or Invalid Attributes
       (Appendix B.3.3));





Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 61]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   6.  canonicalize this object with JCS and compare the result with the
       decoded payload; reject on any difference;

   7.  verify the cryptographic signature using the identified key and
       algorithm; reject on failure;

   8.  on success, consider as intact and authentic only the attributes
       listed in .

       Note: An alert whose and have been removed becomes an unsigned
       alert.  Recipients *SHOULD* apply an explicit policy (rejection
       or marking) to unsigned alerts when a signature is expected.

B.10.  Security Considerations

B.10.1.  Key Protection

   Private keys *MUST* be protected against unauthorized access.
   Implementations *SHOULD* use hardware security modules (HSM) or
   secure enclaves for key storage.

B.10.2.  Algorithm Agility

   To ensure the long-term security of IDMEFv2 alerts, implementations
   *SHOULD* support algorithm agility, allowing migration to newer
   algorithms as cryptographic best practices evolve.  As post-quantum
   signature algorithms (for example ML-DSA, SLH-DSA) are standardized
   and become widely available, they *MAY* be used in place of, or in
   combination with, classical algorithms (for example via hybrid
   signatures).  The parameter facilitates this transition by enabling
   different key types to be identified without changing the IDMEFv2
   data model.

B.10.3.  Replay Attacks

   JWS does not inherently prevent replay attacks.  Implementations
   *SHOULD* include time-based attributes among the signed attributes
   (for example ) and/or rely on transport-layer security.  The use of
   JWT-like / claims is *outside the scope* of the IDMEFv2 model and
   *MUST NOT* be assumed by a generic verifier.

B.10.4.  Key Rotation

   Regular key rotation *SHOULD* be implemented.  The parameter
   facilitates it by allowing the recipient to identify the key used.

B.11.  Complete Example




Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 62]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   {
     "Version": "2.D.V08",
     "ID": "123e4567-e89b-12d3-a456-426614174000",
     "CreateTime": "2024-05-21T14:30:00Z",
     "Type": "Cyber",
     "Category": "Access.Compromise",
     "Confidence": 0.95,
     "Analyzer": {
       "ID": "analyzer01",
       "Name": "Corporate EDR",
       "IP": "192.0.2.10"
     },
     "Source": [ { "IP": "198.51.100.20" } ],
     "SignedAttributes": [
       "Version", "ID", "CreateTime", "Type", "Category",
       "Analyzer.ID", "Analyzer.Name", "Source(0).IP"
     ],
     "Signature": "<valid JWS Compact string>"
   }

   The corresponding canonicalized payload (before base64url encoding)
   is:

   {"Analyzer":{"ID":"analyzer01","Name":"Corporate EDR"},"Category":"Access.Compromise","CreateTime":"2024-05-21T14:30:00Z","ID":"123e4567-e89b-12d3-a456-426614174000","SignedAttributes":["Version","ID","CreateTime","Type","Category","Analyzer.ID","Analyzer.Name","Source(0).IP"],"Source":[{"IP":"198.51.100.20"}],"Type":"Cyber","Version":"2.D.V08"}


   Note: ``Confidence`` and ``Analyzer.IP`` do not appear in the payload because they
   are not listed in ``SignedAttributes``. Signed and verifiable test vectors
   **MUST** accompany the final version.

B.12.  Conformance and Test Vectors

   An implementation is conformant with this annex if it satisfies all
   the *MUST* and *MUST NOT* requirements above.  The final version
   *MUST* provide:

   *  at least one test vector per supported algorithm (Supported
      Algorithms (Appendix B.7.1));

   *  one negative test vector per rejection case (Absent or Invalid
      Attributes (Appendix B.3.3), Consistency (Appendix B.4.3),
      Verification Policy (Appendix B.7.3), Verification Procedure
      (Appendix B.9));

   *  the expected canonicalized payload and signature values.

                     Table 9: enum_alert_entitysector

C.2.  Alert Category

   +======+===============================+============================+
   | Rank | Keyword                       | Description                |
   +======+===============================+============================+
   | 0    | Abuse.Coercion                | The practice of            |
   |      |                               | compelling an individual   |
   |      |                               | to act against their       |
   |      |                               | will by using force,       |
   |      |                               | threats, intimidation,     |
   |      |                               | or extreme dependency,     |
   |      |                               | often for personal or      |
   |      |                               | financial gain.            |
   |      |                               | Examples: an insider       |
   |      |                               | forced by a third party    |
   |      |                               | to disclose credentials,   |
   |      |                               | a person pressured into    |
   |      |                               | handing over access        |
   |      |                               | badges.                    |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 1    | Abuse.Extremism               | The process of             |
   |      |                               | socializing an             |
   |      |                               | individual, often          |
   |      |                               | through psychological      |
   |      |                               | manipulation, to adopt     |
   |      |                               | the beliefs and goals of   |
   |      |                               | a violent extremist        |
   |      |                               | group and become willing   |
   |      |                               | to engage in activities    |
   |      |                               | that support the group.    |
   |      |                               | Examples: online           |
   |      |                               | radicalization through     |
   |      |                               | extremist forums,          |
   |      |                               | recruitment within a       |
   |      |                               | closed community.          |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 2    | Abuse.Grooming                | The deliberate process     |
   |      |                               | of building an emotional   |
   |      |                               | connection and trust       |
   |      |                               | with a person to lower     |
   |      |                               | their inhibitions and      |
   |      |                               | defenses, in order to      |
   |      |                               | psychologically            |
   |      |                               | manipulate them for        |
   |      |                               | purposes such as sexual    |
   |      |                               | abuse, exploitation,       |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 76]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   |      |                               | trafficking, financial     |
   |      |                               | fraud, organizational      |
   |      |                               | coercion, or illicit       |
   |      |                               | insider activity.  Ex:     |
   |      |                               | an adult building trust    |
   |      |                               | with a minor via a         |
   |      |                               | gaming platform, with      |
   |      |                               | repeated "friendly"        |
   |      |                               | contacts near a school;    |
   |      |                               | or a colleague             |
   |      |                               | cultivating a              |
   |      |                               | relationship of            |
   |      |                               | dependency to draw         |
   |      |                               | someone into fraud or      |
   |      |                               | insider dealing.           |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 3    | Abuse.Harassment              | A pattern of unwanted,     |
   |      |                               | intrusive behaviour        |
   |      |                               | (physical, verbal, or      |
   |      |                               | online) directed at a      |
   |      |                               | specific person that       |
   |      |                               | causes fear, distress,     |
   |      |                               | or emotional harm, or      |
   |      |                               | any such                   |
   |      |                               | behaviour—including a      |
   |      |                               | single severe              |
   |      |                               | incident—that creates a    |
   |      |                               | hostile or intimidating    |
   |      |                               | environment.  Ex:          |
   |      |                               | sustained threatening      |
   |      |                               | messages on social         |
   |      |                               | media, repeated            |
   |      |                               | following of an            |
   |      |                               | employee, or a one-off     |
   |      |                               | serious act of             |
   |      |                               | intimidation.              |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 4    | Abuse.Trafficking             | The act of recruiting,     |
   |      |                               | transporting,              |
   |      |                               | transferring,              |
   |      |                               | harbouring, or receiving   |
   |      |                               | a person through force,    |
   |      |                               | fraud, or coercion for     |
   |      |                               | the purpose of             |
   |      |                               | exploitation, such as      |
   |      |                               | forced labour or sexual    |
   |      |                               | servitude.  Ex:            |
   |      |                               | recruitment via fake job   |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 77]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   |      |                               | ads for forced online      |
   |      |                               | scams, transport of        |
   |      |                               | victims across a border.   |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 5    | Abuse.Other                   | Any other incident         |
   |      |                               | involving manipulation     |
   |      |                               | or coercion of people      |
   |      |                               | for harmful purposes       |
   |      |                               | that does not fit into     |
   |      |                               | the specific abuse         |
   |      |                               | subcategories.             |
   |      |                               | Examples: psychological    |
   |      |                               | domination of a remote     |
   |      |                               | worker, confinement of a   |
   |      |                               | person.                    |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 6    | Access.Authorized             | An incident involving      |
   |      |                               | legitimate, approved       |
   |      |                               | physical or digital        |
   |      |                               | access to resources that   |
   |      |                               | may be relevant for        |
   |      |                               | auditing, monitoring, or   |
   |      |                               | establishing a baseline    |
   |      |                               | of normal activity.        |
   |      |                               | Examples: an admin login   |
   |      |                               | during a maintenance       |
   |      |                               | window, an employee        |
   |      |                               | entering a server room     |
   |      |                               | with a valid badge, a      |
   |      |                               | packet accepted through    |
   |      |                               | a firewall.                |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 7    | Access.Backdoor               | The installation or        |
   |      |                               | discovery of a concealed   |
   |      |                               | method of bypassing        |
   |      |                               | normal authentication or   |
   |      |                               | encryption in a computer   |
   |      |                               | system, product,           |
   |      |                               | embedded device or a       |
   |      |                               | building.  Examples: a     |
   |      |                               | hidden SSH key installed   |
   |      |                               | on a server, a concealed   |
   |      |                               | door in a facility.        |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 8    | Access.Cloned                 | The act of creating an     |
   |      |                               | unauthorized copy of a     |
   |      |                               | physical or digital        |
   |      |                               | security credential,       |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 78]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   |      |                               | such as an access card,    |
   |      |                               | to gain illicit entry to   |
   |      |                               | a facility or system.      |
   |      |                               | Examples: a duplicated     |
   |      |                               | RFID badge, a copied key   |
   |      |                               | to a locked cabinet.       |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 9    | Access.Compromise             | An incident where a        |
   |      |                               | legitimate user's          |
   |      |                               | credentials (e.g.,         |
   |      |                               | username/password,         |
   |      |                               | session tokens, API        |
   |      |                               | keys, SSH keys and         |
   |      |                               | biometric spoofing) are    |
   |      |                               | stolen or guessed and      |
   |      |                               | used by an unauthorized    |
   |      |                               | individual to gain         |
   |      |                               | access to systems or       |
   |      |                               | data.  Examples: stolen    |
   |      |                               | VPN credentials reused     |
   |      |                               | by an attacker, a stolen   |
   |      |                               | badge used to enter a      |
   |      |                               | building.                  |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 10   | Access.Escalation             | An incident where a user   |
   |      |                               | or process gains access    |
   |      |                               | rights, permissions, or    |
   |      |                               | capabilities that exceed   |
   |      |                               | those normally assigned,   |
   |      |                               | often to bypass security   |
   |      |                               | restrictions.  Examples:   |
   |      |                               | privilege escalation to    |
   |      |                               | domain admin, a visitor    |
   |      |                               | reaching a restricted      |
   |      |                               | floor.                     |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 11   | Access.Expired                | An event indicating that   |
   |      |                               | an access attempt was      |
   |      |                               | made using credentials,    |
   |      |                               | tokens, or a badge whose   |
   |      |                               | validity period has        |
   |      |                               | ended.  The incident may   |
   |      |                               | be benign (an oversight)   |
   |      |                               | or malicious (deliberate   |
   |      |                               | reuse of expired           |
   |      |                               | credentials to bypass      |
   |      |                               | controls).  Examples ):    |
   |      |                               | a session token past its   |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 79]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   |      |                               | expiration is accepted     |
   |      |                               | by an application due to   |
   |      |                               | a validation flaw,         |
   |      |                               | granting continued         |
   |      |                               | access , an individual     |
   |      |                               | enters a protected area    |
   |      |                               | using an access badge      |
   |      |                               | whose authorization        |
   |      |                               | expired after their        |
   |      |                               | training lapsed .          |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 12   | Access.Forced                 | An incident involving      |
   |      |                               | the use of physical        |
   |      |                               | force to breach a          |
   |      |                               | barrier (e.g., lock,       |
   |      |                               | door, window) or the       |
   |      |                               | repeated systematic        |
   |      |                               | guessing of passwords to   |
   |      |                               | gain unauthorized access   |
   |      |                               | (brute-force attack).      |
   |      |                               | Examples: a brute-force    |
   |      |                               | attack on an SSH           |
   |      |                               | service, forced entry      |
   |      |                               | through a window.          |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 13   | Access.Lost                   | An incident where          |
   |      |                               | physical or digital        |
   |      |                               | access credentials         |
   |      |                               | (e.g., keys, ID badges,    |
   |      |                               | login details) are         |

   |      |                               | used by an unauthorized    |

   |      |                               | lost hardware token, a     |
   |      |                               | misplaced key ring.        |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 14   | Access.Tailgating             | A security breach where    |
   |      |                               | an unauthorized person     |
   |      |                               | follows an authorized      |
   |      |                               | individual through a       |
   |      |                               | secured entry point,       |
   |      |                               | circumventing access       |
   |      |                               | control mechanisms.        |
   |      |                               | Examples: following an     |
   |      |                               | employee through a badge   |
   |      |                               | door, session hijacking    |
   |      |                               | after an authenticated     |
   |      |                               | user.                      |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 80]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 15   | Access.Unauthorized           | Incident involving         |
   |      |                               | unauthorized access to a   |
   |      |                               | restricted building,       |
   |      |                               | room, area, system,        |
   |      |                               | network, application,      |
   |      |                               | account, or data           |
   |      |                               | resource, whether actual   |
   |      |                               | or attempted.  Examples:   |
   |      |                               | a connection to a          |
   |      |                               | database without rights,   |
   |      |                               | entry into a restricted    |
   |      |                               | area, packet denied by a   |
   |      |                               | firewall.                  |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 16   | Access.Other                  | Any other incident         |
   |      |                               | related to physical or     |
   |      |                               | digital access that does   |
   |      |                               | not fit into the           |
   |      |                               | specific access            |
   |      |                               | subcategories.             |
   |      |                               | Examples: access via an    |
   |      |                               | undocumented API, entry    |
   |      |                               | through an unmonitored     |
   |      |                               | gate.                      |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 17   | Anomaly.Burst                 | A sudden, abrupt, high-    |
   |      |                               | amplitude deviation over   |
   |      |                               | a short window, whether    |
   |      |                               | or not followed by a       |
   |      |                               | return to normal.          |
   |      |                               | Examples: a sudden spike   |
   |      |                               | in connections or          |
   |      |                               | requests, a panic          |
   |      |                               | movement in a crowd, a     |
   |      |                               | sudden gust or storm, a    |
   |      |                               | pollution peak.            |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 18   | Anomaly.Chaos                 | A disordered,              |
   |      |                               | unpredictable behavior     |
   |      |                               | with high entropy,         |
   |      |                               | showing no identifiable    |
   |      |                               | pattern or stable          |
   |      |                               | structure.  Examples:      |
   |      |                               | erratic network traffic,   |
   |      |                               | chaotic crowd movement,    |
   |      |                               | atmospheric turbulence,    |
   |      |                               | uncontrolled spread of a   |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 81]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   |      |                               | wildfire.                  |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 19   | Anomaly.Context               | A behavior that is         |
   |      |                               | normal in absolute terms   |
   |      |                               | but abnormal in its        |
   |      |                               | context (location, role,   |
   |      |                               | time, system state,        |
   |      |                               | threat level).             |
   |      |                               | Examples: an admin         |
   |      |                               | logging in from an         |
   |      |                               | unusual country, a         |
   |      |                               | vehicle on a forbidden     |
   |      |                               | lane, ordinary activity    |
   |      |                               | during a crisis, a         |
   |      |                               | normal crowd in a place    |
   |      |                               | where it is not            |
   |      |                               | expected.                  |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 20   | Anomaly.Correlation           | An unusual covariation     |
   |      |                               | between several            |
   |      |                               | entities, signals, or      |
   |      |                               | domains that should not    |
   |      |                               | evolve together, or the    |
   |      |                               | breaking of a normally     |
   |      |                               | expected correlation.      |
   |      |                               | Examples: correlation      |
   |      |                               | between VPN access and     |
   |      |                               | physical access, between   |
   |      |                               | weather and incidents,     |
   |      |                               | between network flows      |
   |      |                               | and crowd behavior; or     |
   |      |                               | conversely, a sensor       |
   |      |                               | losing its expected        |
   |      |                               | correlation.               |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 21   | Anomaly.Deviation             | Any measurable deviation   |
   |      |                               | from an established        |
   |      |                               | behavior, baseline, or     |
   |      |                               | norm, without              |
   |      |                               | presupposing its           |
   |      |                               | temporal shape or          |
   |      |                               | amplitude.  This is the    |
   |      |                               | generic subcategory, to    |
   |      |                               | be used when no other      |
   |      |                               | applies.  Examples: a      |
   |      |                               | user account logging in    |
   |      |                               | from an unusual            |
   |      |                               | location, a crowd whose    |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 82]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   |      |                               | density exceeds the        |
   |      |                               | normal range, an out-of-   |
   |      |                               | season temperature.        |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 22   | Anomaly.Drift                 | A slow, gradual, and       |
   |      |                               | almost imperceptible       |
   |      |                               | deviation that settles     |
   |      |                               | over time and only         |
   |      |                               | becomes visible when       |
   |      |                               | comparing distant time     |
   |      |                               | windows.  Examples: slow   |
   |      |                               | drift of an account's      |
   |      |                               | privileges, gradual        |
   |      |                               | erosion of an              |
   |      |                               | infrastructure,            |
   |      |                               | progressive warming,       |
   |      |                               | slow change in crowd or    |
   |      |                               | traffic composition.       |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 23   | Anomaly.Oscillation           | A repeated alternation     |
   |      |                               | between two or more        |
   |      |                               | states, without            |
   |      |                               | convergence or             |
   |      |                               | stabilisation, over a      |
   |      |                               | given window.  Examples:   |
   |      |                               | flapping of a service,     |
   |      |                               | back-and-forth movement    |
   |      |                               | of a crowd, sensor         |
   |      |                               | oscillation, alternating   |
   |      |                               | weather conditions.        |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 24   | Anomaly.Relation              | A deviation affecting      |
   |      |                               | the set, structure, or     |
   |      |                               | topology of relations      |
   |      |                               | between an asset and       |
   |      |                               | other assets, rather       |
   |      |                               | than the asset's own       |
   |      |                               | value, timing, or          |
   |      |                               | sequence.  Examples: a     |
   |      |                               | workstation that           |
   |      |                               | normally connects to a     |
   |      |                               | stable set of servers      |
   |      |                               | suddenly connects to a     |
   |      |                               | new one; an asset whose    |
   |      |                               | movement pattern shifts    |
   |      |                               | to an entirely new set     |
   |      |                               | of locations.              |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 83]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


   | 25   | Anomaly.Sequence              | An anomaly affecting the   |
   |      |                               | order, succession, or      |
   |      |                               | cadence of normally        |
   |      |                               | expected events, rather    |
   |      |                               | than their value.          |
   |      |                               | Examples: process steps    |
   |      |                               | reversed or skipped,       |
   |      |                               | unusual movement order     |

   |      |                               | heatwave).                 |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 26   | Anomaly.Threshold             | Crossing a predefined      |
   |      |                               | threshold by a simple,     |
   |      |                               | non-statistical rule,      |
   |      |                               | without baseline           |
   |      |                               | analysis.  Examples: a     |
   |      |                               | temperature threshold, a   |
   |      |                               | crowd density threshold,   |
   |      |                               | a connection attempt       |
   |      |                               | threshold, a network       |
   |      |                               | throughput threshold.      |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 27   | Anomaly.Timing                | An anomaly affecting the   |
   |      |                               | moment when an event       |
   |      |                               | occurs, normal in itself   |
   |      |                               | but happening at an        |
   |      |                               | unusual time.  Examples:   |
   |      |                               | a login at an atypical     |
   |      |                               | hour, a crowd arriving     |
   |      |                               | too early or too late,     |
   |      |                               | out-of-season rain,        |
   |      |                               | activity outside           |
   |      |                               | expected hours.            |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+

   |      |                               | analysis.  Examples: an    |
   |      |                               | unclassified deviation     |
   |      |                               | flagged by a detection     |

   | 30   | Availability.DDoS             | An incident where          |
   |      |                               | multiple compromised       |

   |      |                               | flooding a web service,    |

   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 31   | Availability.DoS              | An incident where a        |
   |      |                               | single machine or          |
   |      |                               | network attempts to make   |
   |      |                               | a system, service, or      |
   |      |                               | network resource           |
   |      |                               | unavailable by             |
   |      |                               | overwhelming it with       |
   |      |                               | malicious requests or      |
   |      |                               | traffic.  Examples: a      |
   |      |                               | single-host SYN flood,     |
   |      |                               | jamming of a radio         |
   |      |                               | channel.                   |
   +------+-------------------------------+----------------------------+
   | 32   | Availability.Failure          | An incident caused by      |

   |      |                               | malfunction of hardware    |

   |      |                               | breakdown.                 |

   |      |                               | (CPU, GPU, network         |

   |      |                               | services, etc.) for the    |

Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027                [Page 96]

   |      |                               | without the owner's        |
   |      |                               | consent.  This includes,   |
   |      |                               | but is not limited to,     |

                       Table 10: enum_alert_category

C.3.  Analyzer Category

    +======+===================+======================================+
    | uank | Keyword           | Description                          |
    +======+===================+======================================+
    | 0    | Undetermined      | The analyzer category has not been   |
    |      |                   | determined, or cannot be established |
    |      |                   | from available information.  Ex: an  |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027               [Page 113]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


    |      |                   | analyzer whose type is unknown at    |
    |      |                   | ingestion time.                      |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 1    | APP.BAST          | Bastion Host — A hardened,           |
    |      |                   | intermediary server that provides    |
    |      |                   | secure remote access to a protected  |
    |      |                   | network, acting as a single          |
    |      |                   | controlled entry point.  Ex: an SSH  |
    |      |                   | jump host, a RDP gateway.            |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 2    | APP.DAST          | Dynamic Application Security Testing |
    |      |                   | — Testing a running application from |
    |      |                   | the outside to find security         |
    |      |                   | weaknesses in its runtime behaviour, |
    |      |                   | without access to source code.  Ex:  |
    |      |                   | a scanner probing a live web app for |
    |      |                   | injection flaws.                     |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 3    | APP.IAST          | Interactive Application Security     |
    |      |                   | Testing — Hybrid testing that        |
    |      |                   | instruments a running application    |
    |      |                   | from within (agent-based) while it   |
    |      |                   | is exercised, combining runtime and  |
    |      |                   | code-level visibility.  Ex: an agent |
    |      |                   | inside a Java app reporting tainted  |
    |      |                   | data flows.                          |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 4    | APP.RASP          | Runtime Application Self-Protection  |
    |      |                   | — Security embedded inside a running |
    |      |                   | application that detects and blocks  |
    |      |                   | attacks in real time from within the |
    |      |                   | runtime, independently of the        |
    |      |                   | surrounding infrastructure.  Ex: an  |
    |      |                   | in-app module blocking a SQL         |
    |      |                   | injection attempt.                   |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 5    | APP.SANDBOX       | Sandbox / File Detonation — Isolated |
    |      |                   | execution environment that analyses  |
    |      |                   | the behaviour of suspicious files or |
    |      |                   | URLs and decides whether to raise an |
    |      |                   | alert.  Ex: a suspicious attachment  |
    |      |                   | detonated in a VM to observe its     |
    |      |                   | behaviour.                           |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 6    | APP.SAST          | Static Application Security Testing  |
    |      |                   | — Analysis of application source     |
    |      |                   | code or binaries without executing   |
    |      |                   | them, to find vulnerabilities early  |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027               [Page 114]

Internet-Draft                   IDMEFv2                    October 2026


    |      |                   | in development.  Ex: a linter        |
    |      |                   | flagging unsanitised input in        |
    |      |                   | source.                              |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 7    | APP.SCA           | Software Composition Analysis —      |
    |      |                   | Analysis of third-party dependencies |
    |      |                   | and libraries for known              |
    |      |                   | vulnerabilities, licence issues, and |
    |      |                   | supply-chain risk.  Ex: detecting a  |
    |      |                   | vulnerable open-source component in  |
    |      |                   | a build.                             |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 8    | CLD.CASB          | Cloud Access Security Broker — A     |
    |      |                   | control point between users and      |
    |      |                   | cloud services that enforces         |
    |      |                   | security policy, visibility, and     |
    |      |                   | compliance for cloud usage.  Ex:     |

    |      |                   | movement, presence, or objects using |
    |      |                   | radio waves.  Ex: a ground radar     |
    |      |                   | detecting an intrusion across a      |
    |      |                   | perimeter.                           |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 79   | PHY.SMOKE         | Smoke Detector — Detection of smoke  |
    |      |                   | particles indicating a fire.  Ex: a  |
    |      |                   | smoke detector triggering an alarm   |
    |      |                   | in a server room.                    |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 80   | PHY.SWIR          | Short-Wave Infrared — Imaging in the |
    |      |                   | short-wave IR band (roughly 0.9–1.7  |
    |      |                   | µm), useful for seeing through haze  |
    |      |                   | and some materials.  Ex: a SWIR      |
    |      |                   | camera inspecting contents through a |
    |      |                   | container.                           |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 81   | PHY.VAD           | Voice Activity Detection — Audio     |
    |      |                   | analysis that detects the presence   |
    |      |                   | of human speech in a signal.  Ex:    |
    |      |                   | triggering recording only when       |
    |      |                   | speech is present.                   |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 82   | PHY.VNIR          | Visible and Near-Infrared — Multi-   |
    |      |                   | spectral imaging covering the        |
    |      |                   | visible and near-IR bands.  Ex: a    |
    |      |                   | VNIR camera used in low-light        |
    |      |                   | surveillance.                        |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 83   | PHY.WATER         | Water Leak Detector — Detection of   |
    |      |                   | water presence or leakage in a       |
    |      |                   | monitored area.  Ex: a leak sensor   |



Lehmann & Andrejak        Expires 27 April 2027               [Page 125]

    |      |                   | alerting in a data centre.           |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 84   | SIEM.APM          | Application Performance Monitoring — |
    |      |                   | Analysis of application performance  |
    |      |                   | and availability to detect           |
    |      |                   | degradations, errors, or outages.    |
    |      |                   | Ex: alerting on a spike in response  |
    |      |                   | time for a critical service.         |
    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 85   | SIEM.ETL          | Extract, Transform, Load — Data      |
    |      |                   | pipeline tooling that collects,      |

    | 89   | SIEM.SOAR         | Security Orchestration, Automation   |

    +------+-------------------+--------------------------------------+
    | 90   | SIEM.UPTIME       | Uptime Monitoring — Analysis of      |
